{"results":[{"title":"Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory","content":"# Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory\n## Understand the full billing model for Azure Data Factory\nAzure Data Factory runs on Azure infrastructure that accrues costs when you deploy new resources. It's important to understand that other extra infrastructure costs might accrue.\n### How you're charged for Azure Data Factory\nAzure Data Factory is a serverless and elastic data integration service built for cloud scale. There isn't a fixed-size compute that you need to plan for peak load; rather you specify how much resource to allocate on demand per operation, which allows you to design the ETL processes in a much more scalable manner. In addition, ADF is billed on a consumption-based plan, which means you only pay for what you use.\nWhen you create or use Azure Data Factory resources, you might get charged for the following meters:\n1. Orchestration Activity Runs - You're charged for it based on the number of activity runs orchestrate.\n2. Data Integration Unit (DIU) Hours - For copy activities run on Azure Integration Runtime, you're charged based on number of DIU used and execution duration.\n3. vCore Hours - for data flow execution and debugging, you're charged for based on compute type, number of vCores, and execution duration.\nAt the end of your billing cycle, the charges for each meter are summed. Your bill or invoice shows a section for all Azure Data Factory costs. There's a separate line item for each meter.\n### Other costs that might accrue with Azure Data Factory\nWhen you create resources for Azure Data Factory (ADF), resources for other Azure services are also created. They include:\n1. Pipeline Activity execution\n2. External Pipeline Activity execution\n3. Creation/editing/retrieving/monitoring of data factory artifacts\n4. SSIS Integration Runtime (IR) duration based on instance type and duration\nNote\nYou can assign the same tag to your ADF and other Azure resources, putting them into the same category to view their consolidated billing. ADF tag will be inherited by all SSIS IRs in it. If you change your ADF tag, you need to stop and restart all SSIS IRs in it for them to inherit the new tag, see [Reconfigure SSIS IR](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/manage-azure-ssis-integration-runtime#to-reconfigure-an-azure-ssis-ir) section.\n### Using Azure Prepayment with Azure Data Factory\nYou can pay for Azure Data Factory charges with your Azure Prepayment credit. However, you can't use Azure Prepayment credit to pay for charges for third party products and services including those from the Azure Marketplace.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/plan-manage-costs#understand-the-full-billing-model-for-azure-data-factory"},{"title":"Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory","content":"# Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory\n## Estimate costs before using Azure Data Factory\nUse the [ADF pricing calculator](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/?service=data-factory) to get an estimate of the cost of running your ETL workload in Azure Data Factory. To use the calculator, you have to input details such as number of activity runs, number of data integration unit hours, type of compute used for Data Flow, core count, instance count, execution duration, and etc.\nOne of the commonly asked questions for the pricing calculator is what values should be used as inputs. During the proof-of-concept phase, you can conduct trial runs using sample datasets to understand the consumption for various ADF meters. Then based on the consumption for the sample dataset, you can project out the consumption for the full dataset and operational schedule.\nNote\nThe prices used in this example below are hypothetical and are not intended to imply actual pricing.\nFor example, let’s say you need to move 1 TB of data daily from AWS S3 to Azure Data Lake Gen2. You can perform POC of moving 100 GB of data to measure the data ingestion throughput and understand the corresponding billing consumption.\nHere's a sample copy activity run detail (your actual mileage will vary based on the shape of your specific dataset, network speeds, egress limits on S3 account, ingress limits on ADLS Gen2, and other factors).\n![S3 copy run](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/s3-copy-run-details.png)\nBy using the consumption monitoring at pipeline-run level, you can see the corresponding data movement meter consumption quantities:\n![S3 copy pipeline consumption](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/s3-copy-pipeline-consumption.png)\nTherefore, the total number of DIU-hours it takes to move 1 TB per day for the entire month is:\n1.2667 (DIU-hours) \\* (1 TB / 100 GB) \\* 30 (days in a month) = 380 DIU-hours\nNow you can plug 30 activity runs and 380 DIU-hours into ADF pricing calculator to get an estimate of your monthly bill:\n![S3 copy pricing calculator](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/s3-copy-pricing-calculator.png)","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/plan-manage-costs#estimate-costs-before-using-azure-data-factory"},{"title":"Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory","content":"# Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory\n## Example scenarios\nEstimated pricing for a month (8 hours per day for 30 days):\n| Types | Calculation| \n|  --- | ---  |\n| **Orchestration** (activity run counts in thousands) | 3 activity runs per execution (1 for trigger run, 2 for activity runs).Activity run counts/month = 3 \\* 8 \\* 30 = **720**.Activity run counts in thousand/month = **1** |\n| **Execution** | 1. Data integration unit (DIU) hours: • DIU hours **per execution** = 10 min • Default DIU setting = 4 • DIU hours/month = (10 min / 60 min) \\* 4 \\* 8 \\* 30 = **160**2. External pipeline activity execution hours: • Per execution time: 10 min • External pipeline activity execution hours = (10 min / 60 min) \\* 8 \\* 30 = **40** |\n\n#### Pricing calculator example\nTotal scenario pricing for 30 days: $41.01\n[![Screenshot of the Azure pricing calculator showing the cost estimation for this scenario.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/apply-finops/pricing-calculator-scenario-1.png)](media/apply-finops/pricing-calculator-scenario-1.png#lightbox)\n### Example: Using mapping data flow debug for a normal workday\nThis example shows mapping data flow debug costs for a typical workday for a data engineer. The prices used in the following example are hypothetical and aren't intended to imply exact actual pricing. Read/write and monitoring costs aren't shown since they're typically negligible and won't impact overall costs significantly. Activity runs are also rounded to the nearest 1000 in pricing calculator estimates.\n#### Azure Data Factory engineer\nA data factory engineer is responsible for designing, building, and testing mapping data flows every day. The engineer logs into the Azure Data Factory Studio in the morning and enables the debug mode for data Flows. The default Time to Live (TTL) for debug sessions is 60 minutes. The engineer works throughout the day for 8 hours, so the debug session never expires. Therefore, the engineer's charges for the day are:\n8 hours \\* 8 compute-optimized cores \\* $0.193 per hour per core = **$12.35**","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/apply-finops#example-scenarios"},{"title":"Apache Airflow compute in Fabric","content":"# Apache Airflow compute in Fabric\n## Capacity units and pricing\nMicrosoft Fabric measures compute usage in **Capacity Units (CUs)**. All workloads, including Apache Airflow, use CUs.\nFor specifics about CUs and the pricing model for Apache Airflow pools, see [Apache Airflow job pricing for Data Factory in Microsoft Fabric](https://learn.microsoft.com/fabric/data-factory/pricing-apache-airflow-job).\nFor pricing specifics in your region, see [Microsoft Fabric pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/microsoft-fabric/).\nTip\nCustom pools don't shut down automatically when idle.\nYou pay for the base nodes (5 CUs for small, 10 CUs for large) as long as the pool is running, even if no jobs are running.\nIf you want to save costs during downtime, you can manually pause a custom pool or your whole Fabric capacity. When paused, the pool stops using CUs, but you can't run jobs until you resume it. Resuming takes a few minutes.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/fabric/data-factory/apache-airflow-compute#capacity-units-and-pricing"},{"title":"Save costs for resources with reserved capacity - Azure Data Factory data flows","content":"# Save costs for resources with reserved capacity - Azure Data Factory data flows\n**APPLIES TO:**![](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/applies-to/yes.png) Azure Data Factory ![](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/applies-to/no.png) Azure Synapse Analytics\nTip\n[Data Factory in Microsoft Fabric](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory) is the next generation of Azure Data Factory, with a simpler architecture, built-in AI, and new features. If you're new to data integration, start with Fabric Data Factory. Existing ADF workloads can upgrade to Fabric to access new capabilities across data science, real-time analytics, and reporting.\n1. [Start a Fabric free trial](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/get-started/fabric-trial).\n2. [Upgrade from Azure Data Factory to Data Factory in Microsoft Fabric](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory/migrate-planning-azure-data-factory)\nSave money with Azure Data Factory data flow costs by committing to a reservation for compute resources compared to pay-as-you-go prices. With reserved capacity, you make a commitment for ADF data flow usage for a period of one or three years to get a significant discount on the compute costs. To purchase reserved capacity, you need to specify the Azure region, compute type, core count quantity, and term.\nYou do not need to assign the reservation to a specific factory or integration runtime. Existing factories or newly deployed factories automatically get the benefit. By purchasing a reservation, you commit to usage for the data flow compute costs for a period of one or three years. As soon as you buy a reservation, the compute charges that match the reservation attributes are no longer charged at the pay-as-you go rates.\nYou can buy [reserved capacity](https://portal.azure.com) by choosing reservations [up front or with monthly payments](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/reservations/prepare-buy-reservation). To buy reserved capacity:\n1. To buy a reservation, you must have owner role or reservation purchaser role on an Azure subscription.\n2. For Enterprise subscriptions, **Add Reserved Instances** must be enabled in the [EA portal](https://ea.azure.com). Or, if that setting is disabled, you must be an EA Admin on the subscription. Reserved capacity.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/data-flow-reserved-capacity-overview"},{"title":"Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory","content":"# Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory\n## Understanding Azure Data Factory pricing\nThe chart below explains the general flow of calculating data factory pricing. It shows how to use the [Azure Pricing Calculator](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/) for calculating pricing. Overall, the primary parts to understanding data factory billing involve these costs: **orchestration**, **execution**, **type of integration runtime (IR)**, **data movement (copy)**, and **data flows**.\n1. Check whether your data factory source or sink integration runtime uses managed virtual network (VNET). If so, orchestration and execution are calculated using the Azure managed VNET IR. If not, proceed to the next step.\n2. Confirm whether the source or sink uses the self-hosted integration runtime. If so, orchestration and execution are calculated by the self-hosted IR, and the total cost equals the sum of costs for both orchestration and execution. If not, orchestration and execution are calculated by the Azure IR.\n3. For Azure IR and Azure managed VNET IR, confirm whether you use data flow. If so, the total cost equals the sum of costs for the data flow cluster, orchestration, and execution. Otherwise, the total cost is simply the sum of costs for orchestration and execution.\n![Flow chart showing the process to calculate costs for Azure Data Factory.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/apply-finops/pricing-flow-chart.png)","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/apply-finops#understanding-azure-data-factory-pricing"},{"title":"Save costs for resources with reserved capacity - Azure Data Factory data flows","content":"# Save costs for resources with reserved capacity - Azure Data Factory data flows\nFor more information about how enterprise customers and Pay-As-You-Go customers are charged for reservation purchases, see [Understand Azure reservation usage for your Enterprise enrollment](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/reservations/understand-reserved-instance-usage-ea) and [Understand Azure reservation usage for your Pay-As-You-Go subscription](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/reservations/understand-reserved-instance-usage).\nNote\nPurchasing reserved capacity does not pre-allocate or reserve specific infrastructure resources (virtual machines or clusters) for your use.\n## Determine proper Azure IR sizes needed before purchase\nThe size of reservation should be based on the total amount of compute used by the existing or soon-to-be-deployed data flows using the same compute tier.\nFor example, let's suppose that you are executing a pipeline hourly using memory optimized with 32 cores. Further, let's supposed that you plan to deploy within the next month an additional pipeline that uses general purpose 64 cores. Also, let's suppose that you know that you will need these resources for at least 1 year. In this case, enter the number of cores needed for each compute type for 1 hour. In the Azure portal, search for Reservations. Choose Data Factory > Data Flows, then enter 32 for memory optimized and 64 for general purpose.\n## Buy reserved capacity\n1. Sign in to the [Azure portal](https://portal.azure.com).\n2. Select **All services** > **Reservations**.\n3. Select **Add** and then in the **Purchase Reservations** pane, select **ADF Data Flows** to purchase a new reservation for ADF data flows.\n4. Fill in the required fields and attributes you select qualify to get the reserved capacity discount. The actual number of data flows that get the discount depends on the scope and quantity selected.\n5. Review the cost of the capacity reservation in the **Costs** section.\n6. Select **Purchase**.\n7. Select **View this Reservation** to see the status of your purchase.\n## Cancel, exchange, or refund reservations\nYou can cancel, exchange, or refund reservations with certain limitations. For more information, see [Self-service exchanges and refunds for Azure Reservations](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/reservations/exchange-and-refund-azure-reservations).\n## Need help? Contact us\nIf you have questions or need help, [create a support request](https://portal.azure.com/#blade/Microsoft_Azure_Support/HelpAndSupportBlade/newsupportrequest).","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/data-flow-reserved-capacity-overview"},{"title":"Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory","content":"# Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory\n## Monitor costs\nFollowing are known limitations of per pipeline billing features. These billing meters won't file under the pipeline that spins it, but instead will file under a fall-back line item for your factory.\n1. Data Factory Operations charges, including Read/Write and Monitoring\n2. Charges for [Azure Data Factory SQL Server Integration Services (SSIS) nodes](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/tutorial-deploy-ssis-packages-azure)\n3. If you have [Time to Live (TTL)](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/concepts-integration-runtime-performance#time-to-live) configured for Azure Integration Runtime (Azure IR), Data Flow activities run on these IR won't file under individual pipelines.\n### Monitor consumption at pipeline-run level in Azure Data Factory\nDepending on the types of activities you have in your pipeline, how much data you're moving and transforming, and the complexity of the transformation, executing a pipeline will spin different billing meters in Azure Data Factory.\nYou can view the amount of consumption for different meters for individual pipeline runs in the Azure Data Factory user experience. To open the monitoring experience, select the **Monitor & Manage** tile in the data factory blade of the [Azure portal](https://portal.azure.com/). If you're already in the ADF UX, select on the **Monitor** icon on the left sidebar. The default monitoring view is list of pipeline runs.\nClicking the **Consumption** button next to the pipeline name will display a pop-up window showing you the consumption for your pipeline run aggregated across all of the activities within the pipeline.\n![Pipeline run consumption](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/pipeline-run-consumption.png)\n![Pipeline consumption details](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/pipeline-consumption-details.png)\nThe pipeline run consumption view shows you the amount consumed for each ADF meter for the specific pipeline run, but it doesn't show the actual price charged, because the amount billed to you is dependent on the type of Azure account you have and the type of currency used. To view the full list of supported account types, see [Understand Cost Management data](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/costs/understand-cost-mgt-data).\n### Monitor consumption at activity-run level in Azure Data Factory\nOnce you understand the aggregated consumption at pipeline-run level, there are scenarios where you need to further drill down and identify which is the most costly activity within the pipeline.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/plan-manage-costs#monitor-costs"},{"title":"Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory","content":"# Plan to manage costs for Azure Data Factory\n## Monitor costs\nIn certain cases, you may want a granular breakdown of cost of operations within our factory, for instance, for charge back purposes. Integrating Azure Billing [cost analysis](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/costs/quick-acm-cost-analysis) platform, Data Factory can separate out billing charges for each pipeline. By **opting in** Azure Data Factory detailed billing reporting for a factory, you can better understand how much each pipeline is costing you, within the aforementioned factory.\nYou need to opt in for *each* factory that you want detailed billing for. To turn on per pipeline detailed billing feature,\n1. Go to Azure Data Factory portal\n2. Under *Manage* tab, select *Factory setting* in *General* section\n3. Select *Showing billing report* by pipeline\n4. Publish the change\n![Screenshot of steps to enable per pipeline billing feature.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/per-pipeline-enable.png)\nNote\nThe detailed pipeline billing settings is *not* included in the exported ARM templates from your factory. That means [Continuous Integration and Delivery (CI/CD)](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/continuous-integration-delivery-improvements) will not overwrite billing behaviors for the factory. This allows you to set different billing behaviors for development, test, and production factories.\nOnce the feature is enabled, each pipeline will have a separate entry in our Billing report: It shows *exactly* how much each pipeline costs, in the selected time interval. It allows you to identify spending trends, and notice overspending, if any occurred.\n![Screenshot of example showing granular cost for a pipeline.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/plan-manage-costs/per-pipeline-billing-statement.png)\nUsing the graphing tools of Cost Analysis, you get similar charts and trends lines as shown above, but for individual pipelines. You also get the summary view by factory name, as factory name is included in billing report, allowing for proper filtering when necessary.\nThe change *only* impacts how bills are emitted going forward, and does not change past charges. Please give some time before the change populate to billing report: typically, the change is reflected within 1 day.\nWarning\nBy opting in the per billing setting, there will be one entry for each pipeline in your factory. Please be particularly aware if you have excessive amount of pipelines in the factory, as it may significantly lengthen and complicate your billing report.\n#### Limitations","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/plan-manage-costs#monitor-costs"},{"title":"Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory","content":"# Applying FinOps to Azure Data Factory\n## Budgeting\nWhen planning an Azure Data Factory implementation, it's important to understand and forecast your costs to help build a budget for your ETL and data integration projects.\n![Screenshot of the pipeline monitoring screen highlighting the consumption report button.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/apply-finops/pipeline-monitoring.png)\nSelect on the consumption report button from the pipeline monitoring view to get a snapshot of the units billed for each run.\nOn the monitoring page, you can manually use the consumption report for any pipeline run from a debug or manually triggered run, or even from an automated trigger run.\n![Screenshot of the pipeline run consumption window showing the total units billed for a run.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/media/apply-finops/pipeline-run-consumption.png)\nThe data factory pipeline consumption report provides the estimated units billed. You can run these tests using a debug execution of your pipeline on smaller datasets and then extrapolate your production budget from these estimates.\nThe consumption report provides values in units. To derive a monetary estimate from this, multiply the units’ value in this report by the price in your region based on the Azure pricing calculator. This produces an estimate for that pipeline execution. A best practice is to execute the pipeline several times with different datasets to get a baseline range of costs and use an average of those runs for your budgeting.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/data-factory/apply-finops#budgeting"}]}

# Workflow Orchestration Manager pricing

**APPLIES TO:**![](media/applies-to/yes.png) Azure Data Factory ![](media/applies-to/no.png) Azure Synapse Analytics

Tip

Try out [Data Factory in Microsoft Fabric](/en-us/fabric/data-factory/), an all-in-one analytics solution for enterprises. [Microsoft Fabric](/en-us/fabric/get-started/microsoft-fabric-overview) covers everything from data movement to data science, real-time analytics, business intelligence, and reporting. Learn how to [start a new trial](/en-us/fabric/get-started/fabric-trial) for free!

Important

On January 1, 2026 you'll no longer be able to create new Airflow instances using ADF's Workflow Orchestration Manager. We recommend that you migrate all Workflow Orchestration Manager (Apache Airflow in Azure Data Factory) workloads to [Apache Airflow jobs in Microsoft Fabric](/en-us/fabric/data-factory/apache-airflow-jobs-concepts) before December 31, 2025.

For more information or for support during your migration to Apache Airflow in Microsoft Fabric, contact Microsoft Support.

This article describes the pricing for Workflow Orchestration Manager usage within Azure Data Factory.

## Pricing details

Workflow Orchestration Manager supports either small (D2v4) or large (D4v4) node sizing. Small can support up to 50 DAGs simultaneously, and large can support up to 1000 DAGs. The following table describes pricing for each option:

![Shows a screenshot of a table of pricing options for Workflow Orchestration Manager configuration.](media/airflow-pricing/airflow-pricing.png)
```

Based on the search results, here's the information about Azure Container Apps consumption plan pricing and free grants:

## Free Grant (Monthly)

The first 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds, and 2 million requests per subscription per month are free.

## Pricing Model

Azure Container Apps consumption plan is billed based on per-second resource allocation and requests. Beyond the free grant, you pay for what you use on a per second basis based on the number of vCPU-s and GiB-s your applications are allocated.

## Active vs. Idle Rates

Based on recent pricing information from March 2026:
- Active vCPU-seconds are billed at $0.000024/vCPU-second while replicas are actively handling requests.
- Active GiB-seconds are billed at $0.000003/GiB-second alongside active CPU.
- When minimum replicas > 0, warm replicas are billed at reduced idle rates: $0.000008/vCPU-second and $0.000001/GiB-second.

## Request Charges

HTTP requests are billed at $0.40 per million requests after the 2 million free requests per month.

## Key Scaling Features

An application can be configured to scale to zero replicas when there are no requests or events to process. No usage charges apply when an application is scaled to zero.

> AGENT

This turned up something that changes the answer: **ADF Managed Airflow is effectively off the table.**

Per the Microsoft docs, *"On January 1, 2026 you'll no longer be able to create new Airflow instances using ADF's Workflow Orchestration Manager"* — and today is 2026‑06‑26, so **we can't provision a new one.** Microsoft is steering everyone to **Apache Airflow jobs in Microsoft Fabric** instead. So my earlier "ADF Managed Airflow upgrade" suggestion is dead; the real cloud comparison is **Container Apps vs Fabric Airflow** (vs the current local‑Docker plan).

### Cost comparison (always‑on Airflow — scheduler can't scale to zero)

| Option | Model | Rough monthly cost | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Local Docker** (current plan) | your machine | **$0** | box must be running; poll‑based sensors, no Event‑Grid push |
| **Airflow on Container Apps** | Consumption: $0.000024/vCPU‑s active, **$0.000008 idle**; $0.000003/GiB‑s active, **$0.000001 idle**; 180k vCPU‑s + 360k GiB‑s free/mo | **~$25–75/mo** for 1 vCPU/2 GiB always‑on (+ ~$12–15/mo if you add a managed Postgres for Airflow metadata) | you operate scheduler+webserver+metadata DB; reuses the platform DataQ already runs on |
| **Fabric Apache Airflow jobs** (the ADF successor) | Capacity Units: base **5 CUs (small)/10 CUs (large)** billed continuously unless you pause; needs a Fabric capacity | **~$600+/mo** if never paused (≈5 CU × 730 h × ~$0.18/CU‑h — *approximate*, verify) | turnkey/managed, but heaviest cost; pausable to cut idle spend |
| ~~ADF Managed Airflow~~ | ~~$0.49/node‑h small, $0.99 large~~ | ~~~$358/mo+~~ | **can no longer be created (retired Jan 1 2026)** |

The Container Apps math (1 vCPU / 2 GiB, ~730 h/mo, after the free grant): at mostly‑idle warm rates ≈ **$25/mo**; at fully‑active 24/7 ≈ **$73/mo**. A harness scheduler mostly idles, so expect the low end.

### Takeaway

- **Container Apps is ~10× cheaper than the managed Fabric option** (~$25–75 vs ~$600+/mo) and reuses your existing platform — the trade‑off is you run Airflow yourself (scheduler + webserver + a small metadata Postgres) and it can't scale to zero.
- **Local Docker is still $0** and remains the sensible default for a *disposable* harness — it fully exercises DataQ's Airflow path (HMAC callback + polling).
- If you want cloud/always‑on, **Container Apps is now the cost‑effective choice** (Fabric only if you specifically want fully‑managed and accept the cost).

Numbers are estimates — real bills depend on sizing, region, active/idle split, and the metadata‑DB choice; I computed the Container Apps figures from the documented unit rates.

Want me to record this (ADF Managed Airflow retired → Container Apps as the cloud option if we move off local Docker) in the tracker, and keep the plan as local Docker for now?

Sources:
- [Workflow Orchestration Manager (ADF Managed Airflow) pricing + retirement notice — Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/data-factory/airflow-pricing)
- [Apache Airflow compute in Fabric — capacity units & pricing](https://learn.microsoft.com/fabric/data-factory/apache-airflow-compute)
- [Azure Container Apps pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/details/container-apps/) · [Billing in Azure Container Apps](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/billing)

> AGENT

Done — recorded across the tracker and memory. Here's what the decision changes:

**§1 Terraform grows** (all apply-gated): a shared **Container Apps Environment** hosting both Airflow (scheduler+webserver, LocalExecutor, min‑replicas ≥1, HTTPS ingress) *and* the mockdata Job, plus a **container registry** (both images) and a small **Postgres B1ms** for Airflow metadata. ADF stays managed-cloud.

**A nice bonus:** cloud Airflow has a public HTTPS ingress, so it can receive **real Event‑Grid push** for the file-landing feeds — the local-laptop "poll-only" wrinkle disappears. Event triggers can now be true push.

**One thing to flag — the apply prerequisite list expands.** The Terraform SP can't self-register subscription RPs (we hit that in the plan), so before apply an Owner now needs to register, on top of `Microsoft.Storage` + `Microsoft.DataFactory`:
- `Microsoft.App` (Container Apps)
- `Microsoft.OperationalInsights` (Log Analytics the environment needs)
- `Microsoft.ContainerRegistry`
- `Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL`

I'll fold that into the terraform README when I author these resources.

**Cost reminder:** ~$25–75/mo for the always-on Airflow (1 vCPU/2 GiB) + ~$12–15/mo for the Postgres — vs $0 local Docker and ~$600+/mo Fabric. The disposable `terraform destroy` keeps it bounded.

Buildable-now from here: the **Airflow image** (Dockerfile + DAG skeleton calling the tick CLI / load steps) and the **Container Apps Terraform** (environment + registry + Postgres + Airflow app + the mockdata Job) — authorable now so it's ready the moment you apply, though plan/apply themselves wait on the RP registration.

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/terraform/container_apps.tf",
  "content": "# Container Apps platform — shared host for Airflow (airflow.tf) + the mockdata\n# cron Jobs (mockdata_job.tf). Decision 2026-06-26: Airflow on Container Apps\n# (not local Docker; ADF Managed Airflow was retired for new instances 2026-01-01).\n# Needs subscription RPs: Microsoft.App, Microsoft.OperationalInsights,\n# Microsoft.ContainerRegistry (registered by an Owner — see README).\n\nresource \"azurerm_log_analytics_workspace\" \"harness\" {\n  name                = \"dataq-harness-logs\"\n  location            = var.azure_location\n  resource_group_name = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  sku                 = \"PerGB2018\"\n  retention_in_days   = var.log_retention_days\n  tags                = local.common_tags\n}\n\nresource \"azurerm_container_app_environment\" \"harness\" {\n  name                       = \"dataq-harness-cae\"\n  location                   = var.azure_location\n  resource_group_name        = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  log_analytics_workspace_id = azurerm_log_analytics_workspace.harness.id\n  tags                       = local.common_tags\n}\n\n# Holds the mockdata image (+ a custom Airflow image once §3 bakes DAGs in).\n# admin_enabled = true so the Job pulls with registry creds — avoids an AcrPull\n# role assignment (the Terraform SP is Contributor-only and can't grant roles).\nresource \"azurerm_container_registry\" \"harness\" {\n  name                = \"${var.acr_prefix}${random_string.suffix.result}\"\n  resource_group_name = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  location            = var.azure_location\n  sku                 = \"Basic\"\n  admin_enabled       = true\n  tags                = local.common_tags\n}\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/terraform/postgres.tf",
  "content": "# Airflow metadata database — Postgres Flexible Server (Burstable B1ms, cheapest).\n# Public access + \"allow Azure services\" so the Container Apps Environment reaches it\n# without VNet integration (fine for a disposable harness). Password generated locally\n# (random_password) — never committed; state is gitignored.\n\nresource \"random_password\" \"pg\" {\n  length  = 24\n  special = false # keep the SQLAlchemy URL clean (no %-encoding)\n}\n\nresource \"azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server\" \"airflow\" {\n  name                          = \"dataq-airflow-pg-${random_string.suffix.result}\"\n  resource_group_name           = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  location                      = var.azure_location\n  version                       = \"16\"\n  administrator_login           = var.postgres_admin_login\n  administrator_password        = random_password.pg.result\n  sku_name                      = var.postgres_sku\n  storage_mb                    = 32768\n  auto_grow_enabled             = true\n  public_network_access_enabled = true\n  zone                          = \"1\"\n  tags                          = local.common_tags\n}\n\nresource \"azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server_database\" \"airflow\" {\n  name      = \"airflow\"\n  server_id = azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server.airflow.id\n  collation = \"en_US.utf8\"\n  charset   = \"utf8\"\n}\n\n# start=end=0.0.0.0 is Azure's special \"allow access from Azure services\" rule.\nresource \"azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server_firewall_rule\" \"azure_services\" {\n  name             = \"allow-azure-services\"\n  server_id        = azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server.airflow.id\n  start_ip_address = \"0.0.0.0\"\n  end_ip_address   = \"0.0.0.0\"\n}\n"
}

id: toolu_01TBTtYffMY5PM4W7iyp9MP7
```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/terraform/airflow.tf",
  "content": "# Airflow on Container Apps — scheduler + webserver (LocalExecutor), backed by the\n# Postgres metadata DB. min_replicas = 1 (the scheduler can't scale to zero); HTTPS\n# ingress so Event-Grid (file-landing push) and DataQ can reach it. ~$25-75/mo always-on.\n#\n# Starts on the public airflow image (no DAGs). §3 swaps `airflow_image` for a custom\n# registry image (FROM apache/airflow + COPY dags/) that calls the mockdata tick CLI / loads.\n\nresource \"random_id\" \"fernet\" {\n  byte_length = 32\n}\n\nresource \"random_password\" \"airflow_webserver_secret\" {\n  length  = 32\n  special = false\n}\n\nresource \"random_password\" \"airflow_admin\" {\n  length  = 20\n  special = false\n}\n\nlocals {\n  # Valid Fernet key = url-safe base64 of 32 bytes (swap +/ for -_, keep padding).\n  airflow_fernet_key = replace(replace(random_id.fernet.b64_std, \"+\", \"-\"), \"/\", \"_\")\n\n  airflow_pg_conn = join(\"\", [\n    \"postgresql+psycopg2://\",\n    var.postgres_admin_login, \":\", random_password.pg.result,\n    \"@\", azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server.airflow.fqdn, \":5432/\",\n    azurerm_postgresql_flexible_server_database.airflow.name, \"?sslmode=require\",\n  ])\n\n  # Shared scheduler + webserver env (DRYed into both containers via dynamic blocks).\n  airflow_env = [\n    { name = \"AIRFLOW__DATABASE__SQL_ALCHEMY_CONN\", secret_name = \"pg-conn\" },\n    { name = \"AIRFLOW__CORE__EXECUTOR\", value = \"LocalExecutor\" },\n    { name = \"AIRFLOW__CORE__FERNET_KEY\", secret_name = \"fernet-key\" },\n    { name = \"AIRFLOW__WEBSERVER__SECRET_KEY\", secret_name = \"webserver-secret\" },\n    { name = \"AIRFLOW__CORE__LOAD_EXAMPLES\", value = \"False\" },\n  ]\n}\n\nresource \"azurerm_container_app\" \"airflow\" {\n  name                         = \"dataq-harness-airflow\"\n  container_app_environment_id = azurerm_container_app_environment.harness.id\n  resource_group_name          = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  revision_mode                = \"Single\"\n\n  identity {\n    type = \"SystemAssigned\"\n  }\n\n  secret {\n    name  = \"pg-conn\"\n    value = local.airflow_pg_conn\n  }\n  secret {\n    name  = \"fernet-key\"\n    value = local.airflow_fernet_key\n  }\n  secret {\n    name  = \"webserver-secret\"\n    value = random_password.airflow_webserver_secret.result\n  }\n  secret {\n    name  = \"admin-password\"\n    value = random_password.airflow_admin.result\n  }\n\n  ingress {\n    external_enabled = true\n    target_port      = 8080\n    transport        = \"auto\"\n    traffic_weight {\n      latest_revision = true\n      percentage      = 100\n    }\n  }\n\n  template {\n    min_replicas = 1\n    max_replicas = 1\n\n    # One-shot: migrate the metadata DB + create the admin user before the app starts.\n    init_container {\n      name    = \"airflow-init\"\n      image   = var.airflow_image\n      cpu     = 0.5\n      memory  = \"1Gi\"\n      command = [\"/bin/bash\", \"-c\"]\n      args = [\n        \"airflow db migrate && airflow users create --username ${var.airflow_admin_user} --password \\\"$AIRFLOW_ADMIN_PW\\\" --firstname Demo --lastname Admin --role Admin --email ${var.airflow_admin_user}@example.com || true\",\n      ]\n      env {\n        name        = \"AIRFLOW__DATABASE__SQL_ALCHEMY_CONN\"\n        secret_name = \"pg-conn\"\n      }\n      env {\n        name        = \"AIRFLOW__CORE__FERNET_KEY\"\n        secret_name = \"fernet-key\"\n      }\n      env {\n        name        = \"AIRFLOW_ADMIN_PW\"\n        secret_name = \"admin-password\"\n      }\n    }\n\n    container {\n      name   = \"scheduler\"\n      image  = var.airflow_image\n      cpu    = 0.5\n      memory = \"1Gi\"\n      args   = [\"scheduler\"]\n      dynamic \"env\" {\n        for_each = local.airflow_env\n        content {\n          name        = env.value.name\n          value       = lookup(env.value, \"value\", null)\n          secret_name = lookup(env.value, \"secret_name\", null)\n        }\n      }\n    }\n\n    container {\n      name   = \"webserver\"\n      image  = var.airflow_image\n      cpu    = 0.5\n      memory = \"1Gi\"\n      args   = [\"webserver\"]\n      dynamic \"env\" {\n        for_each = local.airflow_env\n        content {\n          name        = env.value.name\n          value       = lookup(env.value, \"value\", null)\n          secret_name = lookup(env.value, \"secret_name\", null)\n        }\n      }\n    }\n  }\n\n  tags = local.common_tags\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/terraform/mockdata_job.tf",
  "content": "# mockdata file-creation Jobs (§2a) — one Container Apps Job per feed, each on its\n# own cron (var.feed_crons). Runs `python -m mockdata tick --feed <feed>` writing the\n# generated batch + state to ADLS. Source-system simulator, upstream of ADF/Airflow.\n#\n# Auth: ACR pull via registry admin creds; ADLS write via the storage account\n# connection string (AdlsStore reads AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING) — both avoid\n# role assignments the Contributor-only Terraform SP can't create.\n#\n# NOTE: build + push the image before the first scheduled run:\n#   az acr login -n <registry> && docker build -t <login_server>/mockdata:latest . && docker push ...\n# Run `init` once (manually, as a Job execution or locally) to seed state in ADLS.\n\nlocals {\n  adls_root  = \"adls://${azurerm_storage_account.landing.name}/${var.adls_filesystems[0]}\"\n  adls_state = \"${local.adls_root}/state\"\n}\n\nresource \"azurerm_container_app_job\" \"mockdata\" {\n  for_each = var.feed_crons\n\n  name                         = \"mockdata-${each.key}\"\n  container_app_environment_id = azurerm_container_app_environment.harness.id\n  resource_group_name          = data.azurerm_resource_group.dataq.name\n  location                     = var.azure_location\n\n  replica_timeout_in_seconds = 1800\n  replica_retry_limit        = 1\n\n  schedule_trigger_config {\n    cron_expression          = each.value\n    parallelism              = 1\n    replica_completion_count = 1\n  }\n\n  registry {\n    server               = azurerm_container_registry.harness.login_server\n    username             = azurerm_container_registry.harness.admin_username\n    password_secret_name = \"acr-password\"\n  }\n\n  secret {\n    name  = \"acr-password\"\n    value = azurerm_container_registry.harness.admin_password\n  }\n  secret {\n    name  = \"storage-conn\"\n    value = azurerm_storage_account.landing.primary_connection_string\n  }\n\n  template {\n    container {\n      name   = \"mockdata\"\n      image  = \"${azurerm_container_registry.harness.login_server}/mockdata:${var.mockdata_image_tag}\"\n      cpu    = 0.5\n      memory = \"1Gi\"\n      command = [\"python\", \"-m\", \"mockdata\"]\n      args = [\n        \"tick\", \"--feed\", each.key,\n        \"--out\", local.adls_root,\n        \"--state\", local.adls_state,\n      ]\n      env {\n        name        = \"AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING\"\n        secret_name = \"storage-conn\"\n      }\n    }\n  }\n\n  tags = local.common_tags\n}\n"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/mockdata/storage.py",
  "old_string": "class AdlsStore(Store):\n    \"\"\"ADLS Gen2 store for the Container Apps Job.\n\n    Auth via DefaultAzureCredential — managed identity in the Job, `az login` (or\n    AZURE_* env SP) locally. azure-* libs are imported lazily so the package runs\n    with the local store even when they aren't installed.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, account: str, filesystem: str, prefix: str) -> None:\n        from azure.identity import DefaultAzureCredential\n        from azure.storage.filedatalake import DataLakeServiceClient\n\n        self.prefix = prefix.strip(\"/\")\n        svc = DataLakeServiceClient(\n            account_url=f\"https://{account}.dfs.core.windows.net\",\n            credential=DefaultAzureCredential(),\n        )\n        self._fs = svc.get_file_system_client(filesystem)",
  "new_string": "class AdlsStore(Store):\n    \"\"\"ADLS Gen2 store for the Container Apps Job.\n\n    Auth: if AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING is set (the Job passes the storage\n    account connection string — avoids a role assignment the Contributor-only\n    Terraform SP can't grant), use it; otherwise DefaultAzureCredential (managed\n    identity in-cloud, `az login` locally). azure-* libs are imported lazily so the\n    package runs with the local store even when they aren't installed.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, account: str, filesystem: str, prefix: str) -> None:\n        import os\n\n        from azure.storage.filedatalake import DataLakeServiceClient\n\n        self.prefix = prefix.strip(\"/\")\n        conn = os.environ.get(\"AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING\")\n        if conn:\n            svc = DataLakeServiceClient.from_connection_string(conn)\n        else:\n            from azure.identity import DefaultAzureCredential\n\n            svc = DataLakeServiceClient(\n                account_url=f\"https://{account}.dfs.core.windows.net\",\n                credential=DefaultAzureCredential(),\n            )\n        self._fs = svc.get_file_system_client(filesystem)"
}

1	# DataQ harness — Terraform (§1 of HARNESS_TODO.md)
2	
3	Provisions the **datasources** for the live test/demo-data environment (ADR 0021):
4	ADLS Gen2 landing zone, Snowflake DB/warehouse/reader-role, Unity Catalog
5	catalog+schemas, and the ADF factory. Airflow is local Docker (§3), not provisioned here.
6	
7	**Secret-free by design:** every provider reads its credentials from the environment
8	exported by `secrets.sh`. No passwords/keys/tokens live in `.tf` files or outputs.
9	
10	## Usage
11	
12	```bash
13	cd ~/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness
14	source secrets.sh                 # exports ARM_* / SNOWFLAKE_* / DATABRICKS_* 
15	cd terraform
16	terraform init
17	terraform validate
18	terraform plan                    # review — creates nothing
19	terraform apply                   # provisions real, billable resources
20	```
21	
22	### Apply-time prerequisite — subscription resource providers
23	
24	The Terraform SP is Contributor on `dataq-rg` only, so it **cannot register
25	subscription resource providers** (`resource_provider_registrations = "none"`).
26	Before the first `apply`, a subscription **Owner** must register the RPs the
27	harness uses:
28	
29	```bash
30	az provider register --namespace Microsoft.Storage
31	az provider register --namespace Microsoft.DataFactory
32	```
33	
34	`terraform plan` passes without this (it only reads the RG); `apply` of the
35	storage account / data factory will 403 if the RPs aren't registered.
36	
37	After `apply`, copy outputs back into `secrets.sh` blanks:
38	`SNOWFLAKE_DATABASE`, `SNOWFLAKE_WAREHOUSE`, `ADF_FACTORY_NAME` (and switch
39	`SNOWFLAKE_ROLE` to the reader role once Flow A has loaded tables).
40	
41	```bash
42	terraform output                  # connection coordinates for DataQ's connection editor
43	```
44	
45	## Teardown (the env is disposable)
46	
47	```bash
48	terraform destroy
49	```
50	
51	## What's where
52	
53	| File | Resources |
54	|---|---|
55	| `versions.tf`       | Terraform + provider pins, local state backend |
56	| `providers.tf`      | Provider blocks (creds from env) |
57	| `variables.tf`      | Non-secret config (regions, names) |
58	| `main.tf`           | RG data source, random suffix, common tags |
59	| `adls.tf`           | ADLS Gen2 account + `landing`/`raw` filesystems |
60	| `snowflake.tf`      | DB + schema(s) + warehouse + least-priv reader role/grants |
61	| `unity_catalog.tf`  | UC catalog + raw/silver/gold/reference schemas + grants |
62	| `adf.tf`            | Data Factory instance (Flow A pipelines authored later) |
63	| `outputs.tf`        | Connection coordinates (no secrets) |
64	
65	> **No Azure resource names leak into the DataQ repo** (ADR 0010/0013). They live only here.
66	
```

> AGENT

Done — the full Airflow-on-Container-Apps stack is authored and `terraform validate`-clean.

## What I added (`terraform/`)

| File | Resources |
|---|---|
| `container_apps.tf` | Log Analytics workspace + **Container Apps Environment** (shared) + **container registry** (Basic, admin-enabled) |
| `postgres.tf` | **Airflow metadata Postgres** (Flexible Server B1ms) + `airflow` db + allow-Azure firewall; password via `random_password` |
| `airflow.tf` | **Airflow Container App** — init-container (`db migrate` + admin user), scheduler + webserver containers (LocalExecutor), `min_replicas=1`, external `:8080` ingress; Fernet/webserver/admin secrets generated |
| `mockdata_job.tf` | **mockdata cron Jobs** — one `azurerm_container_app_job` per feed over `var.feed_crons`, `tick --feed <name>` → `--out/--state adls://…` |
| `variables.tf` / `outputs.tf` | new vars (image, crons, pg, registry) + outputs (`airflow_url`, `acr_login_server`, `postgres_fqdn`, sensitive admin password, job map) |

## Key design calls (so apply works under the Contributor-only SP)

- **No role assignments** (the SP can't grant RBAC): registry pull uses **admin creds**; the Job writes ADLS via the **storage connection string** — I extended `AdlsStore` to use `AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING` when set, else fall back to managed identity. Both documented as swappable to managed-identity if you apply as an Owner.
- **Valid Fernet key generated in TF** (`random_id` → url-safe base64) — no secret in files, self-contained apply.
- **Airflow starts on the public `apache/airflow:2.10.5` image** (no DAGs yet); §3 swaps `airflow_image` for a registry image with DAGs baked in.

## Verified

- `terraform fmt` + **`terraform validate` clean** (all new schemas resolve — container app with init-container/dynamic-env, container app job with `schedule_trigger_config`, Postgres flexible server).
- `storage.py` change didn't disturb the local path: `init`+`tick` still run, ruff+black clean.

## Apply prerequisites grew (in the README)

Owner must now register **6 RPs**: `Microsoft.Storage`, `Microsoft.DataFactory`, `Microsoft.App`, `Microsoft.OperationalInsights`, `Microsoft.ContainerRegistry`, `Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL`. Post-apply flow documented: copy outputs → build+push the mockdata image → `init` state once → cron Jobs tick.

Everything here is authored/validate-clean but **apply-gated** (RPs + your greenlight). Remaining harness work is now all post-apply: the **§3 reference flows** + the Airflow **DAGs** (and swapping in the custom Airflow image). Want me to scaffold the Airflow DAG skeleton + custom Airflow image next (authorable now), or is this a good place to pause?

> DEVELOPER

So, just to check we are on the same page - can you explain me the flow end to end

> AGENT

Yes — let me lay out the whole thing so we're aligned. The harness exists to stand up a **realistic customer-like data environment** that DataQ monitors, so we can prove DataQ's run paths + orchestration end-to-end (discharging the deferred "live warehouse/file smoke"). Here's the full picture.

## The mental model: three clocks (don't conflate them)

1. **Data-arrival** — the *source systems* keep producing new data.
2. **Load + trigger** — orchestrators pick up that data, load it, and tell DataQ to run.
3. **Check-run** — DataQ executes the suite, persists results, alerts.

## The components & where they run

```
                 ┌─────────────────────── Azure (dataq-rg) ───────────────────────┐
                 │                                                                  │
  CLOCK 1        │   ┌──────────────────────┐                                       │
  (cron)         │   │ Container Apps Env    │   shared host                        │
  ─────────────► │   │  ├─ mockdata Job ×4   │── writes files ─┐                    │
  feed crons     │   │  │   (orders 4×/day,  │                 ▼                    │
  in var.        │   │  │    tracking 2×/day,│        ┌──────────────────┐          │
  feed_crons     │   │  │    inventory daily,│        │  ADLS Gen2        │          │
                 │   │  │    feedback 2-day) │        │  landing/ + raw/  │  + state/│
                 │   │  └─ Airflow            │◄──────►│  (the landing     │          │
                 │   │     (sched+web,        │  reads │   zone)           │          │
                 │   │      Postgres meta)    │        └───────┬──────────┘          │
                 │   └──────────┬─────────────┘                │                     │
                 │              │                              │                     │
  CLOCK 2        │       ADF (managed) + Airflow               │                     │
  (event/sched)  │       load + trigger                        │                     │
                 │              │                              │                     │
                 │     ┌────────┼───────────────┬──────────────┘                     │
                 │     ▼        ▼               ▼ (Flow C: no load — read in place)   │
                 │  Snowflake   Unity Catalog   │                                    │
                 │  (Flow A)    (Flow A ref +   │                                    │
                 │              Flow B medallion)│                                    │
                 └─────────────────────┬────────┼────────────────────────────────────┘
                                       │        │
  CLOCK 3                              ▼        ▼
  (triggered by clock 2        ┌──────────────────────┐
   or DataQ's own W5 cron)     │  DataQ (the app)     │
                               │  CheckRunners →      │
                               │  runs + results +    │
                               │  pipeline_runs →     │
                               │  /results + Teams    │
                               └──────────────────────┘
```

## Follow one batch of data, end to end

1. **Generate (Clock 1).** A mockdata **Container Apps Job** fires on its cron (say `inventory` at 01:00 UTC). It runs `python -m mockdata tick --feed inventory`, reading persisted **state from ADLS** (master pools + climbing id counters + open shipments) so the new batch stays referentially consistent with everything generated before. It writes one batch-suffixed file to the **ADLS landing zone** (and updates state). Deliberate DQ issues (nulls/orphans/stale/volume-drop/…) are seeded and recorded in a manifest so we know exactly where checks *should* fail.

2. **Pick up + load (Clock 2).** An orchestrator notices the new file and acts — **two modalities on purpose**:
   - **Event/file-landing** (orders, tracking): ADF Storage-Event trigger / Airflow sensor fires on arrival → loads into Snowflake/UC.
   - **Schedule/time** (inventory, feedback): ADF Schedule / Airflow cron fires shortly *after* the Job, processes whatever landed in the window.
   This is the path **under test** — it exercises both ADF and Airflow as `OrchestrationProvider` impls, plus DataQ's pipeline-run monitoring + 10-min polling/gap-recovery.

3. **Trigger DataQ.** On successful load, the orchestrator hits DataQ's webhook (`/orchestration/events/{adf,airflow}`). DataQ records the run in **`pipeline_runs`**, and the matching **`trigger_binding`** (`provider`, `pipeline_or_dag_id`, `env` → `suite_id`) dispatches a suite run.

4. **Run checks (Clock 3).** DataQ's Celery worker runs the suite via the right **CheckRunner** (`SnowflakeCheckRunner` / `UnityCatalogCheckRunner` / `FlatFileCheckRunner`), executes the GX expectations, derives severity, and persists rows in **`runs`/`results`** (with `metric_value` for trends).

5. **Surface + alert.** Results show on DataQ's **`/results`** page; severity-banded failures fire **Teams** alerts. Because data keeps arriving on a cadence, the freshness/volume monitors **trend over time** instead of a single green dot.

## The three flows (which run path each batch takes)

- **Flow A — load to warehouse:** file → ADF/Airflow load → **Snowflake** (orders/payments/returns/customer/product — custom-SQL + line-grain + `sum(line_total)=order_total` reconciliation) or **Unity Catalog** plain load (location/promotions/channel — proves the UC runner without medallion). → Snowflake/UC CheckRunner.
- **Flow B — medallion:** file → **UC bronze** → Databricks notebook bronze→silver→gold → DataQ checks on silver/gold (freshness/volume on gold; PII redaction on silver; cross-platform reconcile Snowflake `order_total` ↔ Databricks `gold.daily_revenue`).
- **Flow C — flat-file in place:** file lands in ADLS and **stays** — no load. DataQ's flat-file connection reads it directly with batch resolution (tracking CSV event-append; inventory Parquet daily snapshot). This is the only flow where the Job's file drop *is* the arrival event.

## Why each piece is shaped the way it is

- **Referential consistency across ticks** (shared key pools, stable `tracking_number`, climbing `order_number`) → the only inconsistencies are the *deliberate* ones, so reconciliation/FK/join checks measure real DQ, not generation noise.
- **Reserved-kind shaping** (freshness/volume/anomaly/schema-drift/comparison) → live targets ready the day those monitor kinds ship (ADR 0012/0014), even though v1 only authors GX `expectation` checks.
- **Cadence (Clock 1) is separate from orchestration (Clock 2)** → the Container Apps Job is the *source*; ADF/Airflow are the *observers/loaders* DataQ actually integrates with. Keeps "orchestrator observes, doesn't produce."

**Net:** synthetic retail data is produced on a schedule into a real cloud landing zone, real orchestrators load it and trigger real DataQ suite runs across all three run paths, and DataQ's results/alerting light up with both green checks and the deliberately-seeded failures.

Where does this differ from how you were picturing it — anything you'd want re-shaped (e.g. the flow→datasource mapping, or the event/schedule split)?

shouldn't airflow part of clock 2? your diagram shows it with clock 1

> AGENT

You're right — good catch. That's a real flaw in the diagram: I drew the boxes by **where things are hosted** and let that collide with **what role they play**. Airflow is **Clock 2** (load + trigger), same as ADF. It only *appears* near Clock 1 because it happens to be **hosted in the same Container Apps Environment** as the mockdata Jobs — but hosting ≠ role.

So two separate facts got tangled:
- **Hosting:** the Container Apps Environment physically contains *both* the mockdata Jobs (Clock 1) *and* Airflow (Clock 2). They're co-located to share one environment + registry; that's an infra/cost decision, not a role decision.
- **Role:** mockdata Jobs = Clock 1 (produce). Airflow = Clock 2 (load+trigger), right alongside ADF.

Here's the diagram redrawn **by role/clock**, with hosting shown as just an annotation:

```
CLOCK 1 — DATA ARRIVAL  (cron; the "source systems")
  ┌─ mockdata Job ×4  (orders 4×/day · tracking 2×/day · inventory daily · feedback 2-day)
  │     reads state from ADLS, writes one batch ──►  ADLS Gen2 landing zone (+ state/)
  └─ [hosted in the Container Apps Environment]

                                   │ new file lands
                                   ▼
CLOCK 2 — LOAD + TRIGGER  (event/schedule; the orchestrators DataQ integrates with)
  ┌─ ADF        (managed)                 ─┐  read landing zone
  ├─ Airflow    (hosted in the same        ├─ → load to Snowflake / UC
  │              Container Apps Env +       │  → on success: POST DataQ webhook
  │              Postgres metadata)        ─┘     (/orchestration/events/{adf,airflow})
  │                                            Flow C: NO load — DataQ reads the file in place
  └─ triggers via trigger_bindings → suite run
                                   │
                                   ▼
CLOCK 3 — CHECK-RUN
  DataQ → CheckRunners (Snowflake / UC / FlatFile) → runs + results + pipeline_runs
        → /results page + Teams alerts
```

So the corrected reading: **the Container Apps Environment is a shared host straddling Clock 1 (mockdata Jobs) and Clock 2 (Airflow)** — ADF sits in Clock 2 too but is managed/separate. The data path is strictly Clock 1 → 2 → 3; Airflow's only job is the Clock-2 load+trigger, never producing data.

Okay, and we are providing IAM access to each objects so they can communicate [wherever applicable], right?

> AGENT

Straight answer: **partially — and where we *don't*, it's a deliberate shortcut, not an oversight.** I want to be honest here because it's a real trade-off, not "IAM everywhere, done."

The catch is the **Terraform SP is Contributor on `dataq-rg` only** — and **Contributor cannot create role assignments** (that needs Owner / User Access Administrator). So for the Azure-resource-to-Azure-resource paths that would normally use *managed identity + an RBAC role*, I used **key/credential auth instead**, specifically so `apply` works without an Owner. Where access *doesn't* need an Azure role assignment (Snowflake, Unity Catalog), I did wire proper grants.

Here's every communication path and what it actually uses today:

| Path | Current auth | Azure IAM? |
|---|---|---|
| mockdata Job → **ADLS** (write files+state) | storage **connection string** (Job secret) | ❌ shared key — avoids a Blob Data Contributor role grant |
| Job / Airflow → **ACR** (pull image) | registry **admin creds** (secret) | ❌ key — avoids an AcrPull role grant |
| Airflow → **Postgres** (metadata) | login/password in the conn string | n/a (DB auth, not Azure IAM) — correct |
| Snowflake reader → DB objects | `DATAQ_READER` role + grants | ✅ Snowflake RBAC (in `snowflake.tf`) |
| PAT user → Unity Catalog | `databricks_grants` USE/SELECT | ✅ UC RBAC (in `unity_catalog.tf`) |
| ADF/Airflow → ADLS / Snowflake / UC (load) | **not built yet** (§3) — will be account-key/PAT via connections, or MI+role | ⏳ |
| ADF/Airflow → DataQ webhook | shared-secret URL (ADF) / HMAC (Airflow) | n/a — DataQ's own app auth (ADR 0006/0007) |
| DataQ → Snowflake/ADLS/UC (run checks) | creds in DataQ's SecretStore | n/a — DataQ connection config |

Managed identities *are* provisioned (ADF `SystemAssigned`, Airflow app `SystemAssigned`) — I just **haven't pointed the data-plane access at them**, because that's the part needing role assignments.

**So the decision is yours:**

- **Keep key-based (current):** `apply` works under the Contributor SP with zero Owner involvement. Cost: long-lived shared keys (storage connection string, ACR admin password) live as Container App secrets (resolved from TF state, which is gitignored — not in git, but not short-lived tokens either).
- **Switch to managed identity + RBAC (best practice):** the Jobs/Airflow use their managed identity; I add `azurerm_role_assignment` resources (Storage Blob Data Contributor, AcrPull) and drop the keys. Cleaner and least-privilege — **but the role assignments need an Owner/UAA principal.** I'd guard them behind a `grant_identity_roles` flag (default off so the Contributor apply still works; an Owner flips it on, or runs the equivalent `az role assignment create` commands post-apply).

Given you're asking, I suspect you want the clean IAM model. Want me to **refactor to managed identity + Owner-gated role assignments** (and document the `az role assignment` fallback), or keep the key-based shortcuts for now?

> DEVELOPER

What will be the major drawback of key based vs IAM?

> AGENT

The single biggest one: **a key is a long-lived, account-wide, un-attributable secret you can't cleanly scope or revoke.** Everything else flows from that. Ranked, the real drawbacks of key-based vs IAM:

1. **No least privilege (blast radius).** A storage **connection string = full control of the entire storage account** — every container, read/write/**delete**, plus the power to mint SAS tokens. ACR **admin creds = full push/pull/delete** on the whole registry. IAM scopes to exactly "Blob Data Contributor on *this* filesystem" / "AcrPull (pull-only)". Our Job only needs to *write* `landing/` + `state/` and *pull* one image — the keys hand it god-mode over both.

2. **Long-lived & static.** Account keys / admin passwords **don't expire**. A leak is valid until someone manually rotates. IAM uses **short-lived tokens** (managed-identity tokens auto-rotate, ~hours) fetched per-use — a leaked token dies on its own.

3. **No attribution.** A key isn't tied to a principal, so audit/diagnostic logs can't tell *which* component used it — anyone holding it looks identical. IAM access is a named identity → logs show *who* did *what* (forensics + accountability).

4. **Secret sprawl + distribution.** The key must be copied into every consumer (Container App secret, env var, **Terraform state**). Each copy is an exposure point, and state holds it in plaintext. With IAM there's **no secret to distribute** — the platform injects tokens; the most secure secret is the one that doesn't exist.

5. **Rotation/revocation is coarse and disruptive.** Revoking a leaked key = regenerate it = **breaks every legitimate consumer at once** (Azure gives two keys just to make rolling rotation *possible*, but it's manual ops). IAM: remove one role assignment for one identity, nothing else affected.

6. **Compliance gates.** Many orgs disable storage account keys / ACR admin user via Azure Policy outright — key-based simply won't deploy there; Entra-only (IAM) is the compliant posture.

**But — context matters, and here it's bounded.** This is a *disposable* harness (ADR 0021): one throwaway storage account holding *synthetic* retail data (no real PII, no prod systems), local gitignored state, `terraform destroy` wipes it. So the consequences of every drawback above are contained — which is exactly why the key shortcut is a defensible call to keep `apply` working under the Contributor SP.

**My rule of thumb:**
- **Truly throwaway demo, you control the box → key-based is fine** (what we have).
- **The harness becomes shared / long-lived / a reference others use, or anyone with `gh`/state access is outside your trust circle → switch to IAM** — the least-privilege + clean-revocation properties stop being theoretical.

If you want, I'll do the IAM refactor now (managed identity + Owner-gated role assignments, keys dropped) so it's the clean posture from the start — or we keep keys for the disposable phase and revisit if it outlives the demo.

1	"""DataQ ↔ Apache Airflow DAG-run callback snippet (copy into your DAGs folder).
2	
3	Wire the two callbacks onto each DAG you want DataQ to observe::
4	
5	    from dataq_airflow_callback import on_dataq_success, on_dataq_failure
6	
7	    with DAG(
8	        dag_id="load_finance",
9	        on_success_callback=on_dataq_success,
10	        on_failure_callback=on_dataq_failure,
11	        ...
12	    ):
13	        ...
14	
15	On every DAG-run completion this POSTs a small, HMAC-signed JSON document to
16	DataQ's Airflow event receiver. DataQ records the run in ``pipeline_runs`` and,
17	on success, triggers any suite bound to this DAG (DataQ ADR 0004 / 0007). Both
18	success and failure are reported; only success fires a trigger.
19	
20	Configuration — environment variables, read at call time (e.g. set as Airflow
21	``env`` or exported in the worker environment):
22	
23	    DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL       Full receiver URL, e.g.
24	                            https://dataq.example.com/api/v1/orchestration/events/airflow
25	    DATAQ_WEBHOOK_SECRET    HMAC signing key — the SAME value DataQ stores in Key
26	                            Vault as ``airflow-webhook-secret``.
27	    DATAQ_AIRFLOW_BASE_URL  This Airflow's webserver root, e.g.
28	                            https://airflow.example.com. MUST match the ``base_url``
29	                            of the Airflow connection registered in DataQ (that is
30	                            how DataQ attributes the run). Falls back to Airflow's
31	                            ``[webserver] base_url`` when unset.
32	
33	Design notes: stdlib-only (no extra pip installs), and **fail-safe** — every
34	error is swallowed and logged, so a notification failure can never break your
35	DAG. The HMAC is computed over the exact bytes that are POSTed, matching DataQ's
36	constant-time check on the raw request body.
37	"""
38	
39	from __future__ import annotations
40	
41	import hashlib
42	import hmac
43	import json
44	import logging
45	import os
46	import urllib.error
47	import urllib.request
48	from typing import Any
49	
50	_LOG = logging.getLogger(__name__)
51	
52	_SIGNATURE_HEADER = "X-DataQ-Signature"
53	_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 10
54	
55	
56	def build_payload(
57	    *,
58	    dag_id: str,
59	    run_id: str,
60	    state: str,
61	    base_url: str,
62	    start_date: str | None = None,
63	    end_date: str | None = None,
64	    error: str | None = None,
65	) -> bytes:
66	    """Serialise the event to the exact bytes that are signed and POSTed.
67	
68	    Compact JSON, so the bytes are stable: the signature is computed over this
69	    return value and the same value is sent as the request body — it is never
70	    re-encoded in between (which would invalidate the signature).
71	    """
72	    doc: dict[str, Any] = {
73	        "dag_id": dag_id,
74	        "run_id": run_id,
75	        "state": state,
76	        "base_url": base_url,
77	    }
78	    if start_date:
79	        doc["start_date"] = start_date
80	    if end_date:
81	        doc["end_date"] = end_date
82	    if error:
83	        doc["error"] = error
84	    return json.dumps(doc, separators=(",", ":")).encode("utf-8")
85	
86	
87	def sign(secret: str, body: bytes) -> str:
88	    """HMAC-SHA256 hex digest over the raw body — matches DataQ's receiver."""
89	    return hmac.new(secret.encode("utf-8"), body, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
90	
91	
92	def _post(url: str, body: bytes, signature: str) -> int:
93	    request = urllib.request.Request(  # noqa: S310 — url is operator-configured, not user input
94	        url,
95	        data=body,
96	        method="POST",
97	        headers={"Content-Type": "application/json", _SIGNATURE_HEADER: signature},
98	    )
99	    with urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=_TIMEOUT_SECONDS) as response:  # noqa: S310
100	        return int(response.status)
101	
102	
103	def _iso(value: Any) -> str | None:
104	    """Best-effort ISO-8601 string for an Airflow datetime (pendulum or stdlib)."""
105	    if value is None:
106	        return None
107	    isoformat = getattr(value, "isoformat", None)
108	    if callable(isoformat):
109	        return str(isoformat())
110	    return str(value)
111	
112	
113	def _airflow_base_url() -> str | None:
114	    """Fall back to Airflow's configured webserver base_url, if importable."""
115	    try:
116	        from airflow.configuration import conf
117	
118	        return str(conf.get("webserver", "base_url"))
119	    except Exception:
120	        return None
121	
122	
123	def _notify(context: dict[str, Any], state: str) -> None:
124	    """Build, sign, and POST one DAG-run event. Never raises."""
125	    url = os.environ.get("DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL")
126	    secret = os.environ.get("DATAQ_WEBHOOK_SECRET")
127	    if not url or not secret:
128	        _LOG.warning("DataQ callback skipped: DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL / DATAQ_WEBHOOK_SECRET not set")
129	        return
130	
131	    try:
132	        dag_run = context.get("dag_run")
133	        dag_id = getattr(dag_run, "dag_id", None) or getattr(context.get("dag"), "dag_id", None)
134	        run_id = getattr(dag_run, "run_id", None)
135	        base_url = os.environ.get("DATAQ_AIRFLOW_BASE_URL") or _airflow_base_url()
136	        if not dag_id or not run_id or not base_url:
137	            _LOG.warning(
138	                "DataQ callback skipped: could not resolve dag_id/run_id/base_url from context"
139	            )
140	            return
141	
142	        error = context.get("reason") if state == "failed" else None
143	        body = build_payload(
144	            dag_id=str(dag_id),
145	            run_id=str(run_id),
146	            state=state,
147	            base_url=str(base_url).rstrip("/"),
148	            start_date=_iso(getattr(dag_run, "start_date", None)),
149	            end_date=_iso(getattr(dag_run, "end_date", None)),
150	            error=str(error) if error else None,
151	        )
152	        http_status = _post(url, body, sign(secret, body))
153	        _LOG.info(
154	            "DataQ callback delivered: dag=%s run=%s state=%s http=%s",
155	            dag_id,
156	            run_id,
157	            state,
158	            http_status,
159	        )
160	    except urllib.error.HTTPError as exc:
161	        # DataQ replied non-2xx (e.g. 401 bad signature, 422 malformed). Log the
162	        # status, never raise — the DAG run is already complete and unaffected.
163	        _LOG.warning("DataQ callback rejected: http=%s reason=%s", exc.code, exc.reason)
164	    except Exception:
165	        _LOG.exception("DataQ callback failed (DAG run is unaffected)")
166	
167	
168	def on_dataq_success(context: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
169	    """DAG-level ``on_success_callback`` — report a succeeded DAG run to DataQ."""
170	    _notify(context, "success")
171	
172	
173	def on_dataq_failure(context: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
174	    """DAG-level ``on_failure_callback`` — report a failed DAG run to DataQ."""
175	    _notify(context, "failed")
176

> AGENT

Two separate Postgres instances here — important not to conflate them:

## The Postgres I provisioned ≠ DataQ's database

- **`postgres.tf` (in the harness) = Airflow metadata only.** It backs the Airflow scheduler/webserver (DAG state, task history). It has nothing to do with DataQ's data.
- **DataQ's own application Postgres** — where `runs` / `results` / `pipeline_runs` / `connections` / `suites` live — is **part of DataQ's own deployment, not the harness Terraform.** The harness provisions the *environment DataQ monitors*; DataQ itself (API + worker + its DB + Redis + Key Vault) is a separate deployment (the product's Week-7 deploy).

So: **is DataQ's Postgres Azure-hosted? In a deployed setup, yes** — per the architecture it's **Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server**, alongside DataQ's Container Apps. But it's **not** the Airflow Postgres, and it's **not** in the harness `terraform/` — it belongs to DataQ's deployment stack.

## What your question actually surfaces: where does DataQ *run* during harness testing?

This matters for §3, because the cloud orchestrators (Airflow/ADF) have to **reach DataQ's webhook** (`DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL`) to trigger suites:

| DataQ runs… | Its Postgres | Webhook reachability from cloud Airflow/ADF |
|---|---|---|
| **Local docker-compose** | local container | ❌ not reachable — needs a tunnel (ngrok/Dev Tunnel) for the callback to land |
| **Deployed to Azure Container Apps** (Week 7) | Azure DB for PostgreSQL Flexible Server | ✅ clean public HTTPS URL, cloud-to-cloud |

**My recommendation:** deploy DataQ to Azure Container Apps for the harness runs (it's the Week-7 deploy anyway) — then everything is cloud-native and the orchestrator→DataQ trigger path is real. Keep local docker-compose only for quick dev, with a tunnel if you want to test the callback locally. Either way, the §3 DAGs I author are **parametrized on `DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL`**, so they don't hard-depend on the choice — it only affects live verification.

## §3 kickoff (where we are)

I grounded the trigger wiring: DataQ ships `integrations/airflow/dataq_airflow_callback.py` — stdlib-only, HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body, reads `DATAQ_WEBHOOK_URL` / `DATAQ_WEBHOOK_SECRET` / `DATAQ_AIRFLOW_BASE_URL` from env. So our Flow-A Airflow DAGs just set `on_success_callback=on_dataq_success` + the env vars, and DataQ records the `pipeline_run` + fires the bound suite.

Here's the §3 orchestration matrix I'll build to (key-based throughout):

| Feed | Flow | Datasource | Orchestrator · trigger | Load? | DataQ runner |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| orders, payments, returns, customers, products | A | Snowflake | **ADF · storage-event** | yes (ADLS→Snowflake) | SnowflakeCheckRunner |
| locations, promotions, channels | A | Unity Catalog | **Airflow · schedule** | yes (ADLS→UC) | UnityCatalogCheckRunner |
| sales_events, feedback | B | UC medallion | **Airflow · schedule** → Databricks notebook | bronze→silver→gold | UC runner on silver/gold |
| tracking (CSV), inventory (Parquet) | C | ADLS in place | **no orchestrator load** — DataQ reads in place (event/own schedule) | no | FlatFileCheckRunner |

That gives one event-driven (ADF) + one schedule-driven (Airflow) path per warehouse, exactly the mix you asked for.

**Buildable now (verifiable locally):** the custom **Airflow image** (Dockerfile + snowflake/azure provider deps + DAGs baked in) and the **Flow-A/B DAGs** (parse/import-checkable). **Apply-gated (live comms):** actual ADLS→Snowflake/UC loads + the DataQ callback landing.

Two quick confirms before I author the DAGs: (1) the matrix above look right, and (2) for live runs, are we **deploying DataQ to Azure** (recommended) or keeping it local-with-a-tunnel? I'll start on the Airflow image + Flow-A DAG either way.

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/airflow/dags/_harness.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Shared load helpers for the DataQ-harness Airflow DAGs (§3).\n\nAll auth is key-based (the disposable-harness decision): ADLS via the storage\n**connection string**, Snowflake via user/password, Databricks via PAT. Heavy\nclient libs are imported lazily inside the functions so DAG *parsing* stays cheap\n(the scheduler imports this module on every parse).\n\nReads are \"load the whole landing-zone subdir\": each scheduled run concatenates all\nbatch files for a feed and overwrites the target table — idempotent and simple for a\ndemo. (Production would track a per-file watermark; noted as a follow-up.)\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport io\nimport os\n\nimport pandas as pd\n\nADLS_FILESYSTEM = os.environ.get(\"ADLS_FILESYSTEM\", \"landing\")\n\n\ndef _adls_filesystem():\n    from azure.storage.filedatalake import DataLakeServiceClient\n\n    conn = os.environ[\"AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING\"]\n    return DataLakeServiceClient.from_connection_string(conn).get_file_system_client(\n        ADLS_FILESYSTEM\n    )\n\n\ndef read_feed(subdir: str, fmt: str) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"Concatenate every batch file under <filesystem>/<subdir> into one DataFrame.\"\"\"\n    fs = _adls_filesystem()\n    paths = sorted(p.name for p in fs.get_paths(path=subdir) if not p.is_directory)\n    frames = []\n    for path in paths:\n        data = fs.get_file_client(path).download_file().readall()\n        buf = io.BytesIO(data)\n        frames.append(pd.read_csv(buf) if fmt == \"csv\" else pd.read_parquet(buf))\n    return pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True) if frames else pd.DataFrame()\n\n\ndef load_to_snowflake(df: pd.DataFrame, table: str) -> int:\n    \"\"\"Overwrite a Snowflake table from a DataFrame (write_pandas, auto-create).\"\"\"\n    import snowflake.connector\n    from snowflake.connector.pandas_tools import write_pandas\n\n    conn = snowflake.connector.connect(\n        account=os.environ[\"SNOWFLAKE_ACCOUNT\"],\n        user=os.environ[\"SNOWFLAKE_USER\"],\n        password=os.environ[\"SNOWFLAKE_PASSWORD\"],\n        warehouse=os.environ[\"SNOWFLAKE_WAREHOUSE\"],\n        database=os.environ[\"SNOWFLAKE_DATABASE\"],\n        schema=os.environ.get(\"SNOWFLAKE_SCHEMA\", \"RETAIL\"),\n        role=os.environ.get(\"SNOWFLAKE_ROLE\") or None,\n    )\n    try:\n        df.columns = [c.upper() for c in df.columns]\n        _ok, _chunks, rows, _ = write_pandas(\n            conn, df, table.upper(), auto_create_table=True, overwrite=True\n        )\n        return int(rows)\n    finally:\n        conn.close()\n\n\n_PANDAS_TO_SQL = {\n    \"int64\": \"BIGINT\",\n    \"float64\": \"DOUBLE\",\n    \"bool\": \"BOOLEAN\",\n    \"datetime64[ns]\": \"TIMESTAMP\",\n}\n\n\ndef load_to_uc(df: pd.DataFrame, catalog: str, schema: str, table: str) -> int:\n    \"\"\"Create-or-replace + insert a (small, reference-sized) DataFrame into a UC table.\"\"\"\n    from databricks import sql\n\n    full = f\"`{catalog}`.`{schema}`.`{table}`\"\n    cols = \", \".join(f\"`{c}` {_PANDAS_TO_SQL.get(str(t), 'STRING')}\" for c, t in df.dtypes.items())\n    rows = [\n        tuple(None if pd.isna(v) else v for v in record)\n        for record in df.itertuples(index=False, name=None)\n    ]\n    conn = sql.connect(\n        server_hostname=os.environ[\"DATABRICKS_HOST\"].replace(\"https://\", \"\").rstrip(\"/\"),\n        http_path=os.environ[\"DATABRICKS_HTTP_PATH\"],  # SQL warehouse path\n        access_token=os.environ[\"DATABRICKS_TOKEN\"],\n    )\n    try:\n        cur = conn.cursor()\n        cur.execute(f\"CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE {full} ({cols})\")\n        if rows:\n            placeholders = \", \".join([\"?\"] * len(df.columns))\n            cur.executemany(f\"INSERT INTO {full} VALUES ({placeholders})\", rows)\n        return len(rows)\n    finally:\n        conn.close()\n"
}
